\subsubsection*{Joint subject-blocked Dirichlet-multinomial inference}

The preceding Dirichlet sensitivity changes only reporting. A separate experimental procedure fits cell-type coefficients and biological overdispersion jointly, rather than testing independently regularized subject--cell-type estimates. EC quantification still retains both primer-specific likelihoods and the label-blind pooled baseline, with a small fixed total uniform concentration \(A_q\in\{0.25,1\}\). For each of \(N\) positive-count subject--cell-type aggregates, let \(\widehat{\boldsymbol\psi}_i\in\mathbb R^S\), \(n_i>0\), and \(\mathbf C_i\in\mathbb R^{(S-1)\times(S-1)}\) denote its estimated path proportions, observed EC depth, and conditional ILR covariance. The primary covariance excludes the quantification prior and inverts expected likelihood information on identifiable directions. A separately labeled sensitivity instead uses posterior covariance at \(A_q=0.25\). Positive-count quantification failures or unidentifiable covariance fail the whole block, rather than silently dropping subjects.

Unlike the trace match used for reporting above, this inference approximation matches the ILR covariance in Frobenius norm. With \(\mathbf H\in\mathbb R^{S\times(S-1)}\) the orthonormal ILR basis, define the unit-depth multinomial covariance \(\mathbf K_i\in\mathbb R^{(S-1)\times(S-1)}\), effective depth, and fractional effective counts, \begin{equation}\mathbf K_i=\mathbf H^\top\operatorname{diag}(1/\widehat{\boldsymbol\psi}_i)\mathbf H,\qquad n_i^{\rm eff}=\min\left\{n_i,\frac{\langle\mathbf K_i,\mathbf K_i\rangle_F}{\langle\mathbf K_i,\mathbf C_i\rangle_F}\right\},\qquad \mathbf x_i=n_i^{\rm eff}\widehat{\boldsymbol\psi}_i.\end{equation} Here \(\langle\mathbf U,\mathbf V\rangle_F=\operatorname{tr}(\mathbf U^\top\mathbf V)\). This recovers observed depth for an identity path-to-count map, while ambiguous EC assignment generally reduces effective depth. These are not directly observed path counts.

For \(M\) represented subjects and \(C\) cell types, the full design \(\mathbf X\in\mathbb R^{N\times P}\) contains an intercept, \(M-1\) subject indicators, and \(C-1\) cell-type indicators, with \(P=M+C-1\). Reference-category coefficients \(\mathbf B\in\mathbb R^{P\times(S-1)}\) define means \(\boldsymbol\mu_i\in\mathbb R^S\) through \begin{equation}\boldsymbol\mu_i=\operatorname{softmax}\bigl((\mathbf X\mathbf B)_{i,1},\ldots,(\mathbf X\mathbf B)_{i,S-1},0\bigr),\qquad \boldsymbol\pi_i\sim\operatorname{Dirichlet}(\kappa_b\boldsymbol\mu_i).\end{equation} Gamma-function continuation of the Dirichlet-multinomial likelihood to fractional \(\mathbf x_i\) supplies the same likelihood terms as the reporting objective above, without its population-mean pseudocount term. The positive block-specific biological precision \(\kappa_b\) is estimated separately under the subject-only null and the full alternative, including the multinomial limit when supported. It is not \(A_q\), the production global testing concentration, or a strong prior on cell-type effects. The omnibus likelihood-ratio statistic tests all \((C-1)(S-1)\) cell-type coefficients; the paired version uses \(C=2\). At least four subjects, a full-rank design, and residual observations are required. The analytic chi-square tail is pooled-calibrated using 32 within-subject label-permutation families, with a further 32 families reserved for assessment. Failed fits receive \(p=1\), with unavailable direction, on the fixed eligible family.

Each model reports its own fitted means standardized over the same represented subjects. If \(\boldsymbol\mu_{uc}\in\mathbb R^S\) is the fitted mean for subject \(u\) evaluated at cell type \(c\), descriptive usage and a paired contrast are \begin{equation}\overline{\boldsymbol\mu}_c=\frac1M\sum_{u=1}^M\boldsymbol\mu_{uc},\qquad \boldsymbol\Delta_{ab}=\overline{\boldsymbol\mu}_b-\overline{\boldsymbol\mu}_a.\end{equation} This avoids separately pooling cell types over different subject mixtures. It is model-based standardization, not an exact posterior interval. The approximation discards anisotropic measurement uncertainty, conditions on within-path transcript shares and outside-block abundance, omits uncertainty in the pooled baseline, and treats residual biological compositions as conditionally independent given subject effects. These limitations require count-level null checks rather than relying on label permutations alone.

The expanded common-composition null uses 16 independent EC count draws for each of 128 production-eligible hypotheses, retaining observed primer/depth totals and identical true transcript mixtures across cell types within each subject. The omnibus sample spans 128 blocks and the paired sample 121 blocks; all 2,048 trials per variant remain in each denominator. For omnibus testing, likelihood covariance at \(A_q=0.25\), posterior covariance at \(A_q=0.25\), and likelihood covariance at \(A_q=1\) converge for 1,930, 2,041, and 1,933 trials, respectively. Their calibrated nominal-.05 rejection rates are 5.1\%, 5.8\%, and 5.7\%, compared with raw rates 4.6\%, 6.4\%, and 2.9\%. The paired variants converge for 2,016, 2,046, and 2,016 trials, with calibrated rejection 4.6\%, 5.1\%, and 5.7\%, compared with raw rejection 3.8\%, 4.7\%, and 2.9\%. Calibrated nominal-.01 rejection is 1.4\% for all three omnibus variants and 1.2\%, 1.5\%, and 1.5\% for the paired variants. Dimension/subject strata and repeated rejection by hypothesis are retained in the analysis artifacts rather than relying only on aggregate rates. These are conditional-null diagnostics, not joint-gene FDR certification.

A second count-level screen introduces actual biological variation around the same conditional mean. For subject \(u\), let \(\boldsymbol\psi_u^0\in\mathbb R^S\) be its true path mean and draw independent \(\boldsymbol\pi_{uc}^0\sim\operatorname{Dirichlet}(\kappa_0\boldsymbol\psi_u^0)\) for each cell type \(c\), with \(\kappa_0=20\). Both primers and any technical observations of the same subject--cell-type aggregate share this latent draw. Subject-specific within-path transcript ratios and outside-block mass remain constant across cell types. Thus \(E[\boldsymbol\pi_{uc}^0\mid u]=\boldsymbol\psi_u^0\) does not depend on cell type, although realized subject effects need not vanish. The screen uses the same 128 hypotheses and four count draws, retaining all 512 trials per variant. In the order above, raw paired rejection at nominal .05 is 13.9\%, 14.5\%, and 13.5\%, whereas calibrated rejection is 5.9\%, 5.5\%, and 5.9\%; 504, 512, and 504 fits converge. Raw omnibus rejection is 17.6\%, 20.1\%, and 16.2\%, whereas calibrated rejection is 3.9\%, 4.7\%, and 4.7\%; 482, 510, and 484 fits converge. The raw chi-square reference therefore fails this finite-dispersion screen even though it looked reasonable in the common-composition screen.

The completed subject-split omnibus assessment retains the same 1,069-block control universe. The three calibrated variants yield 10, 10, and 11 reproducible BH blocks, compared with 357 for the refitted current control. Their held-half nominal replication is .709, .723, and .711, and descriptive direction agreement on their own event-BH unions is 33/42, 33/40, and 52/63. These are block-level audit counts, not Table~\ref{tab:ec-glmm-reproducibility}'s gene counts. Replacing calibrated tails with raw chi-square tails increases the corresponding reproducible counts to 61, 69, and 62, but the finite-dispersion null failure prevents treating this as a valid improvement. Median effective depth is approximately 16 per subject--cell-type aggregate; 87--94\% of fitted alternatives reach the multinomial limit, so the real-data fit is not usually applying substantial biological partial pooling. Both the fitted-test approximation and the subsequent calibration contribute to the loss of discovery, and neither calibration alone nor greater uncalibrated discovery establishes an unbeaten strategy. Full-data long-read and comparator-matched paired assessments are evaluated separately before any production change.

The completed full-data maximum-likelihood assessment retains 1,942 eligible control blocks, with 93, 8, and 89 failed experimental fits in the same three-variant order. Each variant's own standardized means selects its largest source-effect contrast before long-read mapping or eligibility checks. The resulting all-tested evaluable families contain 646, 663, and 657 blocks, without restriction to an old paired discovery list. Under continuous raw significance ranking, their long-read \(A_{100}\) values are .628, .641, and .621, with top-100 direction agreement 54/100, 56/100, and 58/100. Ranking by calibrated then raw tails instead gives \(A_{100}=.596,.611,.583\), with agreement 58/100, 59/100, and 59/100. Restriction to the common 633 LR-evaluable blocks does not recover strong agreement, continuous-raw areas are .631, .631, and .619. These omnibus block endpoints are not substituted for the paired-method curves in Figure~\ref{fig:tilgner-sign-agreement-rank}; paired comparator-matched assessment remains separate. This joint maximum-likelihood candidate therefore does not solve the combined split-power and external-agreement problem.

The paired maximum-likelihood assessment is also complete on the frozen comparator-matched gene/pair families. Each of the three variants reproduces zero BH genes on the MAJIQ, scQuint, SUPPA2, primer-aware SUPPA2, hybrid, and paired-rMATS families, and five on the 139-gene LeafCutter family. On the SUPPA2 457-gene, 2,342-pair family, direction agreement on each variant's own selected event union is 34/34, 35/35, and 52/53. These small selected sets do not establish retained accuracy on the older production union. Fresh all-tested long-read mappings yield 2,104, 2,126, and 2,106 evaluable associations. Calibrated-then-raw \(A_{100}\) is .688, .673, and .702, with 61/100, 60/100, and 60/100 directions agreeing. Continuous raw ranking gives areas .729, .719, and .748, with agreement 69/100, 68/100, and 66/100. Thus the paired model also fails to resolve both endpoints; its mapping and ranking families remain distinct from the omnibus assessment above. These exploratory results do not replace the production comparison.

The completed paired split assessment uses the frozen gene--pair families of Table~\ref{tab:ec-glmm-reproducibility}, Simes aggregation within pairs and genes, conjunction across halves, and BH adjustment, with failed tests assigned \(p=1\). All three calibrated joint maximum-likelihood variants yield zero reproduced genes on the MAJIQ, scQuint, SUPPA2, primer-aware SUPPA2, hybrid, and paired-rMATS universes; each yields five on the 139-gene LeafCutter universe. On the 457-gene, 2,342-pair SUPPA2 universe, own event-BH-union direction agreement is 34/34, 35/35, and 52/53, respectively, but those unions contain only 34, 35, and 53 common selected events. These high direction rates on a small selected family do not compensate for lost discoveries or establish unchanged reporting accuracy on the former 1,171-event production union. Full-data paired LR assessment remains separate.

The \href{https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC5200948/}{DRIMSeq methodological study} motivates correcting small-sample dispersion estimation with Cox--Reid-adjusted profile likelihood and sharing dispersion information. We test an unmoderated correction separately from the preceding maximum-likelihood fit. For the full subject--cell-type design, let \(\mathbf I_{BB}(\mathbf B,\kappa)\in\mathbb R^{P(S-1)\times P(S-1)}\) be the observed negative coefficient Hessian. The experimental precision estimate uses a prespecified 21-point grid \(\mathcal K=\{100\,2^j:j=-10,\ldots,10\}\), refitting all mean coefficients at every grid point, \begin{equation}\widehat\kappa_{\rm CR}=\underset{\kappa\in\mathcal K}{\arg\max}\left\{\ell(\widehat{\mathbf B}_\kappa,\kappa)-\tfrac12\log\det\mathbf I_{BB}(\widehat{\mathbf B}_\kappa,\kappa)\right\}.\end{equation} This is a discrete-grid approximation, without interpolation or cross-block moderation. A failed mean fit, coefficient-boundary fit, or non-positive-definite information matrix at any grid point fails the profile. A positive determinant alone is insufficient. Endpoint precision selections remain finite and are recorded, not relabeled exact multinomial fits. The selected precision is then held identical in the null and full mean fits, and the test statistic is the ordinary unadjusted likelihood ratio. The profile penalty is not subtracted from that statistic. Precision is selected afresh for every label-permutation design; a failed null-family fit receives \(p=1\) without discarding the independently successful observed fit.

The observed information includes the second derivative of the softmax. For observation \(i\), define \(\boldsymbol\alpha_i=\kappa\boldsymbol\mu_i\), \(\mathbf J_i=\operatorname{diag}(\boldsymbol\mu_i)-\boldsymbol\mu_i\boldsymbol\mu_i^\top\in\mathbb R^{S\times S}\), and vectors \(\mathbf a_i,\mathbf w_i,\mathbf g_i\in\mathbb R^S\), where \(\operatorname{digamma}\) and \(\operatorname{trigamma}\) are the first and second derivatives of \(\log\Gamma\), \begin{equation}\begin{aligned}a_{is}&=\kappa\{\operatorname{digamma}(\alpha_{is}+x_{is})-\operatorname{digamma}(\alpha_{is})\},\\w_{is}&=\kappa^2\{\operatorname{trigamma}(\alpha_{is}+x_{is})-\operatorname{trigamma}(\alpha_{is})\},\\\mathbf g_i&=\mathbf J_i\mathbf a_i.\end{aligned}\end{equation} The logit Hessian \(\mathbf Q_i\in\mathbb R^{S\times S}\) and coefficient information are \begin{equation}\mathbf Q_i=\operatorname{diag}(\mathbf g_i)-\mathbf g_i\boldsymbol\mu_i^\top-\boldsymbol\mu_i\mathbf g_i^\top+\mathbf J_i\operatorname{diag}(\mathbf w_i)\mathbf J_i,\qquad\mathbf I_{BB}=-\sum_i(\mathbf X_i\mathbf X_i^\top)\otimes\mathbf Q_i^{[-S,-S]}.\end{equation} Here \(\mathbf X_i\in\mathbb R^P\) is the transposed design row, \(\otimes\) denotes a Kronecker product, and \(\mathbf Q_i^{[-S,-S]}\) removes the reference-path row and column. Independent numerical score differentiation validates this information in two-, three-, and four-path examples, including high precision and non-optimal coefficients; tests also check reference-path invariance and failure denominators.

A control generates exact integer Dirichlet-multinomial counts with 12 subjects, type-specific depths from 200 to 2,000, biological precision 20, subject-specific logit offsets, and no type effect. In the four settings \((C,S)=(2,2),(2,3),(5,2),(5,3)\), each with 128 retained trials and all fits converged, the original native maximum-likelihood tests reject at nominal .05 in 17.2\%, 22.7\%, 15.6\%, and 15.6\% of trials. Median alternative precision is 50.6, 48.4, 28.4, and 28.2 instead of the generating 20. Cox--Reid grid selection yields median precision 25 and rejection 6.3\%, 7.8\%, 9.4\%, and 8.6\%. Holding precision at the generating value yields 3.1\%, 3.1\%, 6.3\%, and 6.3\%. Thus dispersion fitting itself contributes to the original inflation, independently of the EC-to-effective-count approximation, but this unmoderated grid correction is not uniformly calibrated. An exploratory reference uses \(F=T/\{(C-1)(S-1)\}\) with numerator degrees \((C-1)(S-1)\) and denominator degrees \((N-P)(S-1)\); its corrected-model rejection is 3.1\%, 4.7\%, 3.9\%, and 7.8\%. This is a heuristic tail, not an exact Dirichlet-multinomial F test. These controls do not assess real-data power, split replication, or long-read agreement. Separate actual-EC null screens retain the same production-eligible hypotheses, refit the baseline, and include a known-latent-precision diagnostic; the generating biological precision need not be the exact precision of fractional effective counts. Neither correction nor tail is adopted in the main results pending those checks and both real-data endpoints.

Two actual-EC correction screens have also completed. The common-composition omnibus screen retains 512 trials across 128 blocks, with 483 observed fits and 15,442/16,384 null-family fits converged. Corrected chi-square and F-reference raw rejection at nominal .05 is 4.7\% and 2.3\%; both give pooled-calibrated rejection 5.1\%. In the finite-dispersion paired screen, 504/512 observed fits converge across 121 blocks. Corrected chi-square and F-reference raw rejection is 11.5\% and 6.4\%, whereas both calibrated rates are 5.5\%; 16,123/16,384 reselected null-family fits converge. Holding precision at the generating latent value 20 yields raw rejection 2.7\% and 0.6\%, versus calibrated rejection 6.1\% and 5.7\%. The corrected fit selects the upper grid endpoint 102,400 in 71.8\% of converged finite-dispersion paired trials, despite latent biological precision 20; median effective depth is 21. This discrepancy is evidence that the effective-count approximation and its fitted precision must be examined, not a reason to regard the endpoint as known biological homogeneity. Aggregate calibrated .05 agreement does not establish calibration in each subject/dimension stratum, and these checks do not establish retained split power or external agreement. The remaining actual-EC screens and real-data paired comparisons are evaluated separately.

The two remaining EC screens retain 512 trials each. Without biological type variation, paired corrected chi-square/F raw rejection is 4.1\%/1.8\%, versus calibrated rejection 3.9\%/4.5\%, with 504 observed fits and 16,124/16,384 null fits converged. Finite-dispersion omnibus corrected chi-square/F raw rejection is 14.5\%/9.0\%, versus calibrated 5.1\%/5.5\%, with 482 observed and 15,418 null fits converged. Known-latent-precision omnibus controls give raw rejection 2.0\%/1.0\% and calibrated 5.7\% for both tails, with 484 observed and 15,488 null fits converged. The finite-dispersion omnibus correction selects its upper precision endpoint in 70.1\% of converged fits and has median effective depth 18.5. The chi-square correction therefore does not repair finite-dispersion calibration, and the F reference improves but does not uniformly repair it. The large discrepancy between fitted and generating precision persists after correcting mean-parameter dispersion bias. Measurement covariance, low-depth dispersion estimation, and direct integration of uncertain compositions are investigated separately below; no real-data endpoint improvement is assumed from these null checks.

\subsubsection*{Measurement covariance and low-depth dispersion controls}

A conditional diagnostic selects 32 production-eligible paired hypotheses and 32 omnibus blocks without screening on discoveries or long-read agreement. For each case, one biological-precision-20 latent transcript mixture is drawn, then held fixed across \(R=32\) independent EC count resamples. Primer totals, compatibility, and missingness remain fixed. The pooled nuisance baseline is re-estimated on every resample. Two sensitivities instead use a label-blind baseline pooled within each subject or the known generating subject baseline; the latter is diagnostic, not an available real-data estimator. For one subject--cell-type aggregate, let \(\widehat{\boldsymbol\psi}^{(r)}\in\mathbb R^S\) be its estimate on resample \(r\), \(\boldsymbol\psi^0\in\mathbb R^S\) its fixed truth, and \(\overline{\boldsymbol\psi}=R^{-1}\sum_r\widehat{\boldsymbol\psi}^{(r)}\). Empirical covariance and mean squared error are \begin{equation}\mathbf V_{\rm emp}=\frac1{R-1}\sum_{r=1}^R(\widehat{\boldsymbol\psi}^{(r)}-\overline{\boldsymbol\psi})(\widehat{\boldsymbol\psi}^{(r)}-\overline{\boldsymbol\psi})^\top,\qquad \operatorname{MSE}=\frac{R-1}{R}\operatorname{tr}(\mathbf V_{\rm emp})+\|\overline{\boldsymbol\psi}-\boldsymbol\psi^0\|^2.\end{equation} Both the propagated Fisher proportion covariance and scalar-matched covariance \(\{\operatorname{diag}(\widehat{\boldsymbol\psi})-\widehat{\boldsymbol\psi}\widehat{\boldsymbol\psi}^{\top}\}/n^{\rm eff}\) have dimension \(S\times S\). Their mean predicted traces are divided by \(\operatorname{tr}(\mathbf V_{\rm emp})\), summarized by a median within each block, then a median across blocks. Only cases with all resamples successfully quantified contribute to the primary covariance summary; failures remain in a separate status table.

All three nuisance strategies complete every resample for 28/32 paired cases and 24/32 omnibus cases, providing 398 and 852 subject--cell-type observations per strategy. The pooled-baseline Fisher/empirical variance ratio is 1.07 for paired and 1.20 for omnibus cases; subject-specific nuisance gives 1.08 and 1.10, and known-subject nuisance gives 1.09 and 1.12. Pooled scalar-matched ratios are 1.10 and 1.21. Median effective depths are approximately 26 and 24. On this small, successfully quantified panel, gross covariance inflation or conditioning on a global nuisance baseline does not explain the preceding precision collapse. This does not rule out problems in rare paths, high-dimensional blocks, incomplete fits, or uncertainty outside the conditional diagnostic.

A separate exact-count control lowers the paired binary depths to 20 and 200, with 12 subjects, true biological precision 20, subject-logit standard deviation .5, and mean logit offset \(-2.2\). Across 128 retained trials, native fixed-subject ML rejection at nominal .05 is 25.8\%, and median fitted precision is infinite. Cox--Reid chi-square/F rejection is 13.3\%/10.2\%, with median precision 50 and 126/128 corrected fits converged; known-precision chi-square rejection is 7.0\%. Weak uniform smoothing with total concentration .25 before fitting fractional effective counts leaves ML rejection at 25.8\% and median precision infinite, while corrected chi-square/F rejection is 13.3\%/9.4\%. Largest-remainder integerization of these weakly smoothed counts recovers the original integer counts and original results. Thus fractional-count continuation and EC covariance mismatch are not necessary for this low-depth dispersion failure. Estimating one nuisance mean per subject remains a possible contributor; the controls do not prove it is the sole cause.

\subsubsection*{Direct-count random-subject partial-pooling prototype}

An experimental binary-path model avoids fractional effective-count responses and integrates subject offsets instead of fitting an independent mean coefficient for every subject. It is not used in the main results, and multi-path blocks are not yet supported. Let \(C\) be the number of cell types, \(\mathbf x_c\in\mathbb R^C\) an intercept plus \(C-1\) reference-type indicators, \(\boldsymbol\beta\in\mathbb R^C\) their coefficients, \(b_u\in\mathbb R\) the subject offset, and \(\pi_{uc}\in(0,1)\) latent path-0 usage, \begin{equation}b_u\sim\mathcal N(0,\sigma_b^2),\qquad \mu_{uc}=\operatorname{logit}^{-1}(\mathbf x_c^\top\boldsymbol\beta+b_u),\qquad \pi_{uc}\mid b_u\sim\operatorname{Beta}(\kappa\mu_{uc},\kappa(1-\mu_{uc})).\end{equation} Positive \(\kappa\) controls biological subject--cell-type variation, and \(\sigma_b>0\) controls shared subject variation. Residual compositions are independent across types conditional on the subject offset; both primers of one aggregate share the same composition. Fixed within-path transcript shares and outside-block abundance come from a label-blind pooled baseline, so their uncertainty and misspecification remain limitations.

For primer \(p\), define \(K_p\)-vectors \(\mathbf h^{(p)},\mathbf g_0^{(p)},\mathbf g_1^{(p)}\) of outside-block EC mass and the two within-path EC masses. Its normalized probabilities, observed \(K_p\)-vector \(\mathbf y_{uc}^{(p)}\), and total \(n_{uc}^{(p)}\) obey \begin{equation}\boldsymbol\rho^{(p)}(\pi)=\frac{\mathbf h^{(p)}+\pi\mathbf g_0^{(p)}+(1-\pi)\mathbf g_1^{(p)}}{\mathbf1^\top\{\mathbf h^{(p)}+\pi\mathbf g_0^{(p)}+(1-\pi)\mathbf g_1^{(p)}\}},\qquad \mathbf y_{uc}^{(p)}\mid\pi_{uc}\sim\operatorname{Multinomial}(n_{uc}^{(p)},\boldsymbol\rho^{(p)}(\pi_{uc})).\end{equation} Let \(L_{uc}(\pi)\) be the product of these primer-specific multinomial likelihoods, and \(f_{\rm B}(\pi;a,b)\) the Beta density. Marginal likelihood is \begin{equation}L(\boldsymbol\beta,\kappa,\sigma_b)=\prod_u\int_{\mathbb R}\phi(b;0,\sigma_b^2)\prod_c\left[\int_0^1L_{uc}(\pi)f_{\rm B}\{\pi;\kappa\mu_{uc}(b),\kappa[1-\mu_{uc}(b)]\}\,d\pi\right]db.\end{equation} Here \(\phi\) is the Gaussian density, and the product over \(c\) uses only observed aggregates. The alternative estimates \(C\) mean coefficients and two variance parameters, rather than \(M+C-1\) fixed-subject mean coefficients. A likelihood ratio tests the \(C-1\) type coefficients, refitting both variance parameters under null and alternative. Population descriptive means integrate \(\operatorname{logit}^{-1}(\mathbf x_c^\top\boldsymbol\beta+b)\) over the fitted Gaussian subject distribution, not over different observed subject mixtures.

The prototype uses Gaussian and normalized Beta quadrature; a prior/evidence-proposal mixture places Beta nodes near both the prior and the EC-supported peak. Proposal counts derived from concentration-.25 path fits affect numerical integration only, not the biological likelihood or prior. When EC likelihoods reduce exactly to pure-path powers and constant terms with a path-independent primer normalizer, Beta-function integration replaces numerical path integration without altering the likelihood. Numerical integration must agree after doubling both orders, with absolute tolerance .001 for each fitted log likelihood and their likelihood ratio; the doubled-order objectives are evaluated without refitting. Inaccurate integration or optimization failure gives \(p=1\). Exploratory bounds are \(\beta_j\in[-8,8]\), \(\kappa\in[.1,10^4]\), and \(\sigma_b\in[.02,2]\); boundary estimates are not assumed to satisfy regular chi-square asymptotics.

An experimental score acceleration differentiates the path integral through posterior expectations instead of evaluating five separate quadratures. For one aggregate at conditional mean \(\mu\), define \(m_0=E[\log\pi\mid\mathrm{EC},\mu,\kappa]\), \(m_1=E[\log(1-\pi)\mid\mathrm{EC},\mu,\kappa]\), \(\eta=\operatorname{logit}(\mu)\), and the digamma function \(\mathcal D(x)=d\log\Gamma(x)/dx\). If \(Z(\eta,\kappa)=\int L_{uc}(\pi)f_{\rm B}\{\pi;\kappa\mu,\kappa(1-\mu)\}\,d\pi\), the exact integral scores are \begin{equation}\begin{aligned}\frac{\partial\log Z}{\partial\eta}&=\kappa\mu(1-\mu)\{m_0-m_1-\mathcal D(\kappa\mu)+\mathcal D[\kappa(1-\mu)]\},\\ \frac{\partial\log Z}{\partial\log\kappa}&=\kappa\{\mu[m_0-\mathcal D(\kappa\mu)]+(1-\mu)[m_1-\mathcal D\{\kappa(1-\mu)\}]+\mathcal D(\kappa)\}.\end{aligned}\end{equation} These are scalar conditional scores, subsequently averaged over the Gaussian subject posterior and propagated through the mean design. Pure-path integrals have exact digamma differences. For general ECs, Beta shapes below two instead use centered numerical logit/log-precision differences with step .001, since accurate likelihood integration does not imply accurate quadrature of singular logarithmic moments. This hybrid score changes numerical evaluation, not the likelihood; its actual-EC accuracy and speed are checked against the original numerical-score pilot before scaling up.

The initial exact-count control uses 12 subjects, two cell types, depths 20 and 200 divided between primers, true \(\kappa=20\), Gaussian subject standard deviation .5, and baseline logit \(-2.2\). Subject offsets are redrawn independently for each trial, unlike the preceding fixed-subject control. With 96 Gaussian nodes and the strengthened objective/ratio check, 125/128 null trials converge; nominal-.05 rejection is 8/128 (6.25\%), and median alternative precision is 24.85. A strong-effect control with type logits shifted by \(-.8\) and \(+.8\) converges for 116/128 trials and rejects 115/128 (89.8\%) at nominal .05. Failed or inaccurate fits remain in both denominators. A six-subject paired null converges for 118/128 trials and rejects 6/128 (4.7\%), but its median alternative precision rises to 39.97 and its failed fits preclude treating the aggregate rate as a calibration certificate. A five-type, 12-subject binary null converges for 127/128 and rejects 10/128 (7.8\%), with median alternative precision 22.61. These limited synthetic results motivate an actual-EC accuracy/runtime pilot, not a claim of established calibration, retained split power, improved long-read agreement, or production readiness. Unsupported multi-path blocks must remain failed \(p=1\) hypotheses in any subsequent full-family comparison.

The numerical-score actual-EC pilot retains four independently selected binary paired hypotheses under observed counts, a common-composition null draw, and a biological-precision-20 null draw. Observed/common/biological cases pass optimization and doubled-quadrature checks in 3/4, 4/4, and 3/4 cases; median fitting times are 84, 101, and 85 seconds. None rejects at nominal .05, but four cases are not a calibration or power assessment. The failed observed and biological cases have quadrature discrepancies .0056 and .0482, exceeding the .001 tolerance despite optimizer success. These are numerical failures, not evidence that those effects or biological variation vanish. Remaining accuracy failures, boundary precision selection, and runtime are investigated before any full-family split or long-read comparison of this prototype.

A numerical sensitivity replaces the importance mixture with direct normalized Gauss--Jacobi quadrature under the Beta prior. This changes integration, not the latent model, likelihood, or chi-square reference. Centered differences of the actual quadrature retain the numerical logit and log-precision scores, including at singular Beta endpoints. All four observed, common-composition, and biological-null pilot cases then pass the same accuracy gate, with median fitting times 49, 44, and 46 seconds. This repairs the two initial pilot failures, but concentrated likelihoods can still require more path nodes.

An exact batching optimization shares probability and quadrature grids across subjects with identical cell-type design rows. For cell type \(c\), let \(N_c\) be its number of observed subjects, \(Q_b,Q_\pi\in\mathbb N\) the two quadrature orders, \(L=Q_bQ_\pi\), \(\mathbf Y_c^{(p)}\in\mathbb R_+^{N_c\times K_p}\) the primer-specific count matrix, \(\mathbf R_c^{(p)}\in[0,1]^{K_p\times L}\) the normalized EC probabilities on the shared grid, and \(\mathbf o_c\in\mathbb R^{N_c}\) fixed likelihood offsets. All row-level grid log likelihoods are evaluated by \begin{equation}\boldsymbol\ell_c=\sum_p\mathbf Y_c^{(p)}\log\mathbf R_c^{(p)}-\mathbf o_c\mathbf1_L^\top\in\mathbb R^{N_c\times L}.\end{equation} The logarithm is elementwise. Counts are not pooled, and the separate subject integrals and primer normalizers are unchanged. Identical complete logit rows are grouped exactly, without rounding. Relative to unbatched prior quadrature, median matched-case speedups are 16.2, 16.8, and 17.2 for observed, common-composition, and biological cases. Maximum p-value and fitted-usage differences are below \(4\times10^{-10}\) and \(2\times10^{-11}\), respectively. Median batched fitting times are 2.8, 2.5, and 2.4 seconds. These four-case timing results are not a full-data runtime benchmark.

The broader panel retains 32 independently selected covered binary hypotheses and two count draws per null, giving 64 trials per scenario. Importance-mixture scores pass 46/64 common-composition and 39/64 biological-null fits, with no nominal-.05 calls. Direct prior quadrature, batched or unbatched, passes 50/64 and 54/64, with 0/64 and 3/64 calls. Failed cases remain \(p=1\), so these rejection rates do not establish calibration. Separately doubling each integration order identifies subject-integration failures in 14 common-composition and eight biological cases, versus zero and two path-integration failures. The median true smaller-path mean is .090 and .079, respectively; thus the panel is not composed solely of near-absent paths. These diagnostics motivate a higher-order subject integration check on the identical selected family, rather than accepting failure-induced conservatism.

The completed higher-order check uses 192 subject and 64 path nodes, keeping all selected cases and count seeds unchanged. It passes 64/64 common-composition and 62/64 biological-null fits, with 0/64 and 3/64 nominal-.05 calls and median fitting times approximately 19 seconds in each scenario. The remaining two failures are subject-integration discrepancies, with maximum .0083; maximum path-integration discrepancy is below \(4\times10^{-7}\). Thus increased subject accuracy recovers most missing fits, but neither this small binary panel nor its conservative strict-null rate establishes general calibration or full-data performance.

An additional accuracy check selects eight binary-path blocks from the multilevel omnibus family, including two- and five-type designs. The initial implementation passes seven cases per scenario because it rejects a primer that detects only one path. Conditional on that primer's total, its EC probabilities are independent of path usage, so a zero endpoint normalizer does not invalidate the likelihood at interior compositions. The corrected implementation permits supported interior compositions, treats proportional endpoint maps as constant likelihood factors, and ignores unobserved zero-mass primer maps. With 192 subject and 64 path nodes, all eight observed, common-composition and biological-precision-20 cases pass the accuracy gate; native nominal-.05 calls are 1/8, 0/8 and 1/8. Median fitting times are approximately 29, 32 and 23 seconds, respectively. This extends the numerical pilot beyond two types, not beyond two paths, and does not establish full-family calibration, split power or LR agreement.

\subsubsection*{Independent-block nuisance challenge}

A separate controlled challenge asks whether reads elsewhere in a gene can create a local effect when within-path transcript mixtures are held fixed. It isolates model misspecification rather than EC measurement covariance or finite biological dispersion. Four transcripts form the Cartesian product of binary blocks A and B. For subject \(u\) and type \(c\in\{0,1\}\), define A and B usage \(a_{uc},b_c\in(0,1)\), a shared subject offset \(h_u\sim\mathcal N(0,.3^2)\), scalar logit effects \(\gamma_A,\gamma_B\in\mathbb R\), and the transcript mixture \(\boldsymbol\theta_{uc}\in\Delta^3\), \begin{equation}\begin{aligned}a_{uc}&=\operatorname{logit}^{-1}\{h_u+\gamma_A(2c-1)\},\qquad b_c=\operatorname{logit}^{-1}\{\gamma_B(2c-1)\},\\ \boldsymbol\theta_{uc}&=\bigl(a_{uc}b_c,a_{uc}(1-b_c),(1-a_{uc})b_c,(1-a_{uc})(1-b_c)\bigr)^\top.\end{aligned}\end{equation} Transcript order is A0B0, A0B1, A1B0, A1B1. When \(\gamma_A=0\), target A usage is identical across types within each subject, even if B changes. There is no additional subject--type biological residual. Both primer-specific read maps have seven distinct read-origin classes, two A-inclusion junctions, an A-skipping junction, an A-exclusive exon, two B-specific classes, and an unrelated constitutive exon. Each trial has 12 subjects, two types, and 100/200 gene reads per primer and observation. Known opportunity weights differ between primers and transcripts. The label-blind mean of the generating transcript mixtures supplies a diagnostic oracle baseline, eliminating nuisance-baseline estimation noise as an explanation.

All three read selections use the same binary random-subject Beta EC likelihood-ratio test with 96 subject nodes, 32 path nodes, and the individual-objective/ratio accuracy gate. The first selection uses all gene classes with fixed within-A transcript shares; the second uses only A-origin junction and exon classes; the third uses only A-origin junction classes. Each read is assigned to one origin class. These classes can share a transcript-compatible set but are not merged, unlike conventional transcript EC counts. For the selected A-origin maps, columns are identical within each A path. Consequently their unnormalized mass is \(a_{uc}\mathbf v_0^{(p)}+(1-a_{uc})\mathbf v_1^{(p)}\), where \(\mathbf v_s^{(p)}\in\mathbb R_+^{K_{\rm local}}\) is the known local opportunity vector for A path \(s\), and neither mass nor its local normalizer depends on B usage. This identity explains why the local-origin likelihood removes B as a nuisance in this particular design. It must not be assumed for arbitrary primer opportunities.

Across 64 independently redrawn trials per scenario, all fits pass numerical checks. With \(\gamma_A=\gamma_B=0\), each selection makes one nominal-.05 call. When only B changes, \(\gamma_A=0,\gamma_B=2.2\), the all-gene fixed-mixture model makes 64/64 false target calls and has mean A-effect bias \(-.110\); local junction-plus-exon and junction-only tests each make 2/64 calls, with mean biases .0053 and .0038. With a positive A effect \(\gamma_A=.3\) and the same B change, all three selections reject in 64/64 trials and have the correct direction, but their mean effect biases are \(-.0506,.0033,.0053\), respectively. This strong-effect design does not establish superior power from adding exonic reads. It demonstrates that extra gene reads can worsen a local estimate if their within-path nuisance structure is frozen, despite correctly specified primer maps and successful numerical fitting. An independent change elsewhere in a gene could reproduce across short-read splits while disagreeing with the true local long-read effect. The challenge does not determine how much of the observed real-data discrepancy this mechanism explains.

The remedies suggested by this challenge are to allow within-path transcript composition to change independently of the target block, to fit independent effects for other gene blocks, or to retain read-origin information and use a target-local likelihood whose primer opportunities are verified. A conventional transcript EC identifier does not recover genomic read origin, and summing exon and junction feature counts can count the same read twice. Thus selecting local reads cannot be implemented by relabeling existing gene ECs or naively summing coverage tracks. These controlled results and the existing-estimator nuisance sensitivity are retained separately from production and the full split/long-read benchmark in \path{analyses/independent_block_nuisance}.

An additional controlled sensitivity uses the existing fixed-share and free-transcript quantifiers, followed by the same unmoderated paired mean test of fitted ILRs, without production pooled calibration. With only B changing, fixed shares at total path concentration 1 or 32 again yield 64/64 false calls, with mean A-effect biases \(-.109\) and \(-.090\). Free within-path shares at concentration 1 yield 4/64 calls and mean bias .0097. Each quantifier yields 1/64 calls when both blocks are null. Every requested trial converges after the covariance repair described below. This identifies a useful nuisance-model correction in the controlled design; it does not validate the free quantifier's biological-null calibration, establish preserved full-data split power, or select it using long-read outcomes.

The initial free-share sensitivity exposed a numerical covariance failure, not a failed abundance fit. Near-zero nuisance transcript abundances shrink both Fisher information and target derivatives in global transcript-ILR coordinates; relative eigenvalue truncation then marked the target unidentifiable in every trial, despite successful optimization. The experimental free-share fitter now computes the same working covariance directly in linear simplex-tangent coordinates. Let \(T\) be the number of transcripts, \(\mathbf U\in\mathbb R^{T\times(T-1)}\) an orthonormal sum-zero basis, \(\mathbf D^{(p)}\in\mathbb R_+^{K_p\times T}\) a primer map, \(\mathbf q^{(p)}=\mathbf D^{(p)}\boldsymbol\theta\in\mathbb R_+^{K_p}\), \(z_p=\mathbf1^\top\mathbf q^{(p)}\), \(\mathbf V_p=\mathbf D^{(p)}\mathbf U\in\mathbb R^{K_p\times(T-1)}\), and \(n_p\) the observed primer count total. Expected likelihood information is \begin{equation}\mathbf I_{\rm lin}=\sum_p n_p\left\{\frac{\mathbf V_p^\top\operatorname{diag}(1/\mathbf q^{(p)})\mathbf V_p}{z_p}-\frac{\mathbf V_p^\top\mathbf1\mathbf1^\top\mathbf V_p}{z_p^2}\right\}\in\mathbb R^{(T-1)\times(T-1)}.\end{equation} Define the path-incidence matrix \(\mathbf M\in\{0,1\}^{S\times T}\), path masses \(\mathbf m=\mathbf M\boldsymbol\theta\in\mathbb R_+^S\), path ILR basis \(\mathbf H_S\in\mathbb R^{S\times(S-1)}\), and \(\mathbf G=\operatorname{diag}(1/\mathbf m)\mathbf M\mathbf U\in\mathbb R^{S\times(T-1)}\). The target Jacobian is \(\mathbf J=\mathbf H_S^\top\mathbf G\in\mathbb R^{(S-1)\times(T-1)}\). The same first-order path-prior information adds \(A\mathbf G^\top\{\operatorname{diag}(\boldsymbol\psi)-\boldsymbol\psi\boldsymbol\psi^\top\}\mathbf G\), and the same working transcript-prior information adds \(\epsilon\mathbf U^\top\operatorname{diag}(1/\boldsymbol\theta)\mathbf U\), where \(A\) is total path concentration and \(\epsilon=10^{-4}\) is total transcript concentration. Propagation uses \(\mathbf J\mathbf I^+\mathbf J^\top\), retaining the target-null-space identifiability check. Interior regression tests verify equivalence to the old-coordinate pullback, and a boundary regression verifies recovery of finite target covariance. The abundance objective, optimizer, prior, and fitted means are unchanged. This numerical repair does not turn the working covariance into an exact posterior covariance or certify behavior at a true parameter boundary.

A subsequent real-design nuisance challenge changes within-path transcript shares across cell types while preserving every subject's target path masses and outside-block abundance. Independently generated type-specific log-normal tilts have standard deviation one and are shared across subjects; within each path they are renormalized to its original mass before sampling actual counts from the observed primer maps and depths. Thirty-two covered production-eligible paired hypotheses are selected without discovery or long-read screening, with two count draws each. Fixed-share and free-share fits are assessed on the same selected family at path concentrations 1 and 32, retaining failures as \(p=1\). This challenge also includes multi-path blocks. It is a diagnostic of nuisance misspecification, not a full split/long-read comparison; its results are not assumed in the main findings.

The completed real-design screen passes every fit but does not validate free-share testing. With within-path tilts, both fixed-share and free-share concentration-1 tests reject 11/64 target nulls (17.2\%) after pooled calibration; both concentration-32 tests reject 19/64 (29.7\%). In no-tilt controls on the same hypothesis family, concentration-1 rejection is 9/64 fixed-share and 8/64 free-share, while concentration-32 rejection remains 19/64 for both. Only 11/32 hypotheses have multiple transcripts in at least one path; within-path tilting cannot change the other 21 singleton-path cases. Among the 22 multiplet-path trials, concentration-1 rejection changes from 2/22 to 4/22 for fixed shares and from 2/22 to 5/22 for free shares. These small differences are not evidence of a real-design advantage for the free model. The controlled four-transcript example identifies a structural limitation, but the real-design controls show that residual quantification/testing bias exists even without a within-path change. Neither nuisance flexibility alone nor this covariance repair resolves the split/long-read discrepancy. Full outputs and structural strata are retained in \path{analyses/within_path_type_stress}; no variant is promoted to production.

\subsubsection*{Omnibus regression and quantification diagnostics}

We compare the existing null-variance Wald statistic with residual-based trace F, Pillai, and maximum-coordinate F statistics, on both testing ILRs and concentration-1 descriptive proportions. Each regression retains subject fixed effects. Let \(\mathbf Y\in\mathbb R^{N\times D}\) be the responses, \(\mathbf X\in\mathbb R^{N\times P}\) the full design, and \(Q\) the number of independently tested cell-type columns. For proportions, \(D=S-1\) and \(\mathbf Y\) is projected onto an orthonormal simplex contrast basis to remove its sum-to-one redundancy. Write \(\mathbf E\in\mathbb R^{D\times D}\) for the full-model residual cross-product and \(\mathbf H_E\in\mathbb R^{D\times D}\) for the reduction in residual cross-product from the subject-only null model. The trace statistic is \begin{equation}F_{\mathrm{trace}}=\frac{\operatorname{tr}(\mathbf H_E)/(QD)}{\operatorname{tr}(\mathbf E)/\{(N-P)D\}}.\end{equation} Its analytic reference assumes spherical response covariance. Pillai's trace \(\operatorname{tr}\{(\mathbf H_E+\mathbf E)^{-1}\mathbf H_E\}\) instead accounts for full response covariance. The maximum-coordinate statistic is only an ordering statistic until multiplicity is calibrated. Scalar cases recover ordinary ANOVA; numerical tests also verify Pillai against an independent MANOVA implementation.

The existing Wald implementation estimates its residual scale under the subject-only null. Actual cell-type differences therefore enter that scale; this can limit the statistic for strong signals, although it is not by itself proof of incorrect calibration. Residual-based alternatives use the fitted alternative's residual variation. Each sensitivity uses 32 within-subject permutation families for pooled calibration and 32 independently drawn families for held-out null assessment. Independent Gaussian biological-null simulations additionally vary response covariance and cell-type residual variance. Permutation-based assessment alone is insufficient when cell-type residuals are not exchangeable, so a further sensitivity uses CR2-adjusted subject-cluster sandwich covariance and subject-level wild residual sign flips. The wild procedure preserves each subject's residual vector and cell-type variance pattern rather than permuting its labels. Its maximum-coordinate analytic tail is not interpreted as a global p-value.

A separate quantification sensitivity replaces relative path perturbations with absolute ILRs and profiles the represented block's abundance against outside-block isoforms. For block mass \(g_i\in(0,1)\), within-path transcript shares \(\xi_{ts}\), and outside-block shares \(\xi_{t0}\), the transcript mixture is \(\theta_{it}=g_i\psi_{is}\xi_{ts}\) for transcript \(t\) in path \(s\), and \(\theta_{it}=(1-g_i)\xi_{t0}\) otherwise. The shares remain fixed to the pooled baseline; \(g_i\) is fitted separately for each subject--cell-type observation without a mass prior, and its uncertainty is profiled when obtaining the path covariance. Absolute coordinates avoid excluding valid optima through bounds on perturbations of nearly zero baseline path probabilities. A synthetic count regression verifies recovery of a binary usage increase despite decreasing total block abundance, and a multi-path identity-mapping case recovers its expected smoothed proportions and nuisance mass. This is an experimental fitter, not a change to the primary production likelihood.

Full-data omnibus sensitivities remap every tested block to the long-read source. For each reporting estimator, the strongest contrast among the source's mapped cell types is selected using short-read effect magnitude before source completeness, depth, or direction checks. All-tested and BH-selected rank curves are reported separately, and the archived production omnibus p-values are evaluated under the same remapping as a control. These new curves are not silently substituted for Figure~\ref{fig:tilgner-sign-agreement-rank}, whose original source-evaluable discovery-pair selection differs. Results and fitted effect comparisons are retained in \path{analyses/path_reporting_omnibus}; no sensitivity is promoted solely because it improves long-read agreement.

We additionally combine all eligible archived paired tests within a block, without screening them by significance. For \(J_b\) tested cell-type contrasts and their marginal calibrated p-values \(p_{bj}\), Bonferroni uses \(\min\{1,J_b\min_jp_{bj}\}\), Simes uses \(\min_j\{J_bp_{b(j)}/j\}\), and ACAT uses the Cauchy-combination implementation already used elsewhere in the benchmark. Bonferroni does not require independence if the marginal p-values are valid; Simes needs suitable dependence conditions and ACAT has an asymptotic tail justification. Independently seeded marginal null families are not a valid joint-subject calibration for these combinations. The paired tests estimate variance separately for each contrast, avoiding the omnibus common-variance assumption. Their tested cell-type-pair coverage can differ from that of the multilevel regression, so comparisons retain the common block universe and do not claim that unsupported contrasts have been tested. A separate reporting sensitivity chooses the smallest analytic-tail paired contrast among the source's mapped cell types, before checking long-read depth or direction, instead of choosing the largest descriptive effect. Reporting arrays are explicitly taken from the concentration-1 regression control for every statistic, including the wild and paired-combination statistics, so their ordering in a file cannot silently substitute concentration-32 arrays.

On the 975 blocks supported by every alternative in both subject halves, the current statistic gives 363 reproducible BH blocks, trace F at concentration 32 gives 360, and Pillai gives 330. Lowering testing concentration to 1 gives 74 with ILR trace F and 43 with proportion-space Pillai. The paired combinations give 171 (Bonferroni), 190 (Simes), and 234 (ACAT), while the wild maximum-coordinate sensitivity gives no reproducible BH block. Thus none improves the discovery--replication trade-off. For a fixed design, the current null-variance statistic and trace F are monotone functions of the same signal-to-residual ratio, explaining why pooled calibration largely removes the apparent analytic-reference improvement. Profiling nuisance block mass and using absolute ILRs gives 358 versus 357 reproducible blocks for its refitted null-variance control on the larger 1,069-block regression-only intersection; it does not provide a reporting or long-read improvement sufficient to adopt that fitter.

The Gaussian null audit generates zero cell-type coefficients in ILR space. For scalar responses with unequal cell-type variances, the current statistic and trace F each reject 19/150 nulls (12.7\%) at level .05 after permutation calibration. The wild sensitivity rejects 11/150 (7.3\%), and the Bonferroni and Simes paired combinations each reject 8/150 (5.3\%). These finite simulations identify a heteroscedasticity concern; they do not establish exact calibration of the production EC-derived test. In particular, zero ILR mean effects need not imply equal mean proportions under unequal variances, so proportion-space results are not interpreted as a clean test of that ILR null. The paired-combination Gaussian checks do not include EC assignment, smoothing, or EB-strength selection and therefore do not certify the actual marginal production p-values. Separate held-out permutation families assess calibration-pool generalization rather than resolving this biological-null limitation.

\subsection*{Alternative smoothing targets and path-estimate uncertainty}

Table~\ref{tab:path-eb-scaling} compares the production testing-level selector with discarded count-predictive objectives and the former fixed add-two rule.

The earlier count-predictive EB analysis is retained as a diagnostic. A baseline-centered spike-and-slab count model selected fixed total concentration 16, but applying only that scalar to uniform-centered testing was not a coherent transfer of one prior. Applying a common baseline-centered prior in both stages did not preserve power: an isotropic Gaussian prior on ILR perturbations selected precision 32 and produced only 23 and 11 fold BH calls, while a heavier-tailed symmetric Dirichlet prior on relative path fold changes selected concentration 64 and produced only 31 and 14. Both had pooled null rejection near 0.05 and no null-family BH calls. Shrinking every subject--cell-type composition toward one common biological center therefore attenuates the contrasts, regardless of local curvature or tail weight. Uniform-centered count-predictive evidence instead selected concentrations 2--4 and also lost replication. These failures motivate the production testing-level target, for which selection and testing use the same uniform-centered estimator and null/non-null objective.

Second, if \(I_{bmc}\in\mathbb S_+^{S_b-1}\) is the expected EC likelihood information and \(C(\bm\psi)=\operatorname{diag}(\bm\psi)-\bm\psi\bm\psi^{\mathsf T}\), the conditional Laplace covariance was estimated as \[V_{bmc}=\left\{I_{bmc}+\alpha_bS_bB_b^{\mathsf T}C(\widehat{\bm\psi}_{bmc})B_b\right\}^{-1}\in\mathbb S_+^{S_b-1}.\] For a paired contrast, \(W_{bm}=V_{bm1}+V_{bm2}\in\mathbb S_+^{S_b-1}\) and the measurement-error model was \(\widehat{\bm d}_{bm}\mid\bm d_{bm}\sim N(\bm d_{bm},\lambda W_{bm})\), \(\bm d_{bm}\sim N(\bm\mu_b,\tau_b^2I_{S_b-1})\), where \(\lambda\geq0\) is shared within cross-fitting strata and \(\tau_b^2\geq0\) is fitted by REML. The analogous condition model scaled each mouse's covariance before multivariate GLS.

At \(\lambda=1\), the fold-specific cell-type analyses made 684 and 524 BH calls, but independently replicated genes fell to 28, 10, and 11 against the three comparators. Cross-fitted REML profiles over \(\lambda\in\{0,0.001,0.003,0.01,0.03,0.1,0.3,1,3\}\) selected zero in every split, full cell-type, and full condition stratum. The conditional EC covariance therefore did not explain held-out mouse-to-mouse error beyond the fitted biological variance and harmed the discovery tail when imposed at unit scale. Approximate uncertainty in \(\widehat{\bm\delta}\), \(\widehat{\bm y}\), and \(\widehat{\bm d}\) remains available as a sensitivity but is not carried into the production test.

\subsection*{Pairwise Tealeaf sensitivity analyses}

The corrected tangent-null Laplace EC GLMM was first rerun on the complete 25-UMI pairwise candidate manifests. Against LeafCutter, relaxed-coverage MAJIQ Heterogen, and scQuint it had 12, 19, and 18 replicated genes, compared with 25, 38, and 57, while its held-half nominal replication rates were 0.867, 0.828, and 0.828. The global latent model therefore retained stable signals but did not realize the expected local-path power advantage.

The direct local-path estimator without smoothing had unstable boundary estimates. Table~\ref{tab:path-alpha-sensitivity} reports the final common-pipeline comparison of no smoothing and 0.5--128 pseudo-observations per path. The earlier native-test screens used to develop variance moderation are superseded by this comparison. Dropping nuisance isoforms and ECs with only nuisance support, combining globally and locally conditioned p-values, restricting to two-path blocks, and replacing Simes with Cauchy gene aggregation did not improve calibrated replication and were rejected. These analyses show that boundary stabilization and cross-block variance moderation, rather than discarding ambiguous EC information, account for the final power gain.

The concentration curve suggests an adaptive rule in which weakly supported effects receive stronger shrinkage while strong effects receive weaker shrinkage. We tested two calibrated diagnostics on the independent long-read validation. The first combined all eight calibrated concentration-specific p-values with a Cauchy statistic. The second used the zeta-2 p-value when it was below a pre-specified 0.01 threshold and otherwise used the zeta-32 p-value, followed by one BH correction; the same switch was applied to every signed-label null family. Both retained approximately 5\% nominal null rejection and fewer than one BH call per null family on average. Neither improved pooled directional replication: Cauchy gave 430/740 (58.1\%), the switch gave 449/772 (58.2\%), and fixed zeta=4 and zeta=32 gave 290/491 (59.1\%) and 410/696 (58.9\%), respectively. MAJIQ Heterogen gave 86/139 (61.9\%) on the same validation. Choosing the concentration from the observed effect magnitude can produce a larger apparent rate in this dataset, but that threshold was tuned on the validation data and is not a valid production procedure without incorporating the selector into null calibration. We therefore retain a single global testing concentration and use weak regularization only for post-selection effect reporting.

\subsection*{Depth-filtered split sensitivity}

Filtering the pairwise split benchmark at a median gene depth of 100 modeled UMIs reduces the jointly converged Tealeaf fits to 1,101 block-pair tests from 202 genes in fold 0 and 564 tests from 94 genes in fold 1. The intersections with LeafCutter, relaxed-coverage MAJIQ, and scQuint then contain only 172, 364, and 337 gene-pair tests from 36, 63, and 61 genes. Tealeaf has 4, 11, and 11 replicated genes on these intersections, compared with 9, 17, and 11 for the respective comparators. Although the retained 77-test label-swap subset has a 0.0519 nominal rejection rate and no p-value below 0.01, its resolution is too low and its coverage loss is too severe for this filter to define the main genome-wide comparison. It is retained only as a depth sensitivity.

Lowering the inclusion threshold from 25 to 10 modeled gene UMIs and replacing the separate block tests with one unrestricted isoform-composition test per gene and cell-type pair expands the two split manifests to 22,453 tests from 2,503 genes and 16,753 tests from 2,238 genes. On the shared universes, this joint-gene sensitivity has 23 versus 29 replicated genes against LeafCutter, 66 versus 35 against relaxed-coverage MAJIQ, and 53 versus 104 against scQuint. Its held-half nominal replication is 0.780, 0.839, and 0.842, exceeding the corresponding comparator rates of 0.684, 0.659, and 0.776. The larger universe therefore recovers reproducible signal and exceeds MAJIQ's discovery count, but it does not dominate the other comparators. More importantly, only 946 of 1,000 paired-label-swap fits jointly converge, 86, 21, and 6 have p-values below 0.05, 0.01, and 0.001, and three survive BH at 0.05. This low-depth joint test is too anti-conservative for production.

We also tested the requested gene model in which every annotated block contributes an independent fixed-effect subspace, with linear dependencies among overlapping block contrasts removed before fitting. At the 25-UMI screen, its shared-universe replicated-gene counts are 12, 19, and 18 against LeafCutter, relaxed MAJIQ, and scQuint, below both the respective comparator counts of 24, 38, and 57 and the unrestricted joint-gene sensitivity on the same screen. In its 1,000-test label-swap audit, 966 fits jointly converge, rejection rates are 0.0704, 0.0217, and 0.00414, and two tests survive BH. Independent block effects therefore reduce power without improving calibration over the production block tests, so this model is not retained as a fitting option.

\subsection*{Nonbootstrap Laplace sensitivity}

Table~\ref{tab:laplace-lrt-sensitivity} compares the analytic Laplace LRT and BIC Bayes-factor approximation with the completed mouse bootstrap analysis. A Laplace fit is retained only when the infinity norms of its scaled outer gradient and random-effect mode score are each at most \(10^{-4}\). Scaling the objective and gradient by the absolute initial objective changes neither the optimum nor the likelihood ratio, but prevents count depth from setting the numerical line-search scale.

The multinomial analyses share 247 BH calls, and the logistic-normal analyses share 204. Among these matched tests, the BF threshold selects 160 and 128 events, of which 142 and 116 are bootstrap BH calls. Applying the nominal chi-square reference to the existing variational-fit null simulations gives 0.05 rejection rates 0.0389 and 0.0374, indicating mild conservatism for that diagnostic rather than inflation.

The logistic-normal alternative estimates its observation-noise standard deviation at the lower numerical bound in 758 of 4,438 jointly converged fits. Because this nuisance variance is then on a boundary, its Wilks reference is less regular. The multinomial LRT is the cleaner nonbootstrap sensitivity, while the BF threshold supplies a smaller high-evidence subset; neither supersedes the empirically calibrated primary result without the mouse comparison above.

The independent human analysis gives the results in Table~\ref{tab:human-laplace-lrt}. The recommended multinomial likelihood was rerun with closed-form pseudobulk likelihood derivatives, 12 posterior-mode Newton steps, and implicit mode differentiation on the first attempt, with 30 steps on continuation attempts; the logistic-normal table entry is the previous supporting sensitivity. The condition calls span 137 distinct blocks and 135 genes; 176 of 193 also exceed the BIC BF threshold. EX1, EX2, EX5, EX3, and EX4 contribute 62, 34, 21, 19, and 14 condition calls, respectively, with the remainder distributed across other neuronal and glial cell types.

The BF and BH counts need not be ordered: \(\mathrm{BF}>10\) is an uncorrected evidence threshold whose BIC penalty depends on tested dimension and subject count, while BH controls a genome-wide frequentist error rate.

\subsection*{Computational benchmarks}

Table~\ref{tab:method-resources} compares aggregate worker time and peak resident memory for the broad cell-type and condition campaigns. Aggregate worker time is the sum of elapsed time over analysis tasks, so it is independent of queue delay and parallelism. Peak memory is the maximum for one worker. The accounting starts from each method's prepared analysis input and includes model fitting and method-specific result construction, but excludes shared read processing. Tealeaf uses the same ordinary multinomial EC GLMM, closed-form pseudobulk likelihood derivatives, and Laplace LRT in both datasets. Each first attempt uses 12 posterior-mode Newton updates and implicit differentiation, failed-convergence fits receive a 30-update continuation, and compiled objectives are reused within bounded gene-and-shape groups. On a complete 72-test human condition shard, implicit differentiation reduced elapsed time from 79.7 to 37.1 minutes and peak memory from 6.88 to 6.66 GiB relative to differentiating through the Newton updates, with the same 69 jointly converged tests and median absolute statistic difference below \(10^{-12}\). Gene-group compilation reuse reduced a second shard benchmark from 37.9 to 37.1 minutes. Complete analytic-derivative cell-type and condition reruns used 51.0 mouse worker hours and 4.81 GiB peak worker memory, and 313.3 human worker hours and 8.25 GiB; each broad campaign is 3.3-fold slower than scQuint, compared with 7.8-fold before the derivative change. Human condition fitting uses finer shards than cell-type fitting because retaining compiled shapes across hundreds of heterogeneous condition tests in one process exceeds the worker memory budget; the mathematical model, initialization, optimizer, tolerances, and continuation are unchanged. The human run has 4.3-fold more tests than mouse and its median test has five rather than three isoforms, 26 rather than nine ECs, and five rather than two block paths; these larger likelihoods account for most of the remaining dataset difference. Lower-precision and early-stopping experiments did not preserve accuracy or reduce real-data elapsed time, so neither is used for the reported fits.

A matched optimization study retained L-BFGS as the production outer optimizer. Pure projected Adam at two learning rates failed the joint outer-gradient and posterior-mode convergence criteria on nearly every tested fit and changed some LRT statistics by hundreds; an Adam warm-up followed by L-BFGS recovered convergence but reached different local optima and changed statistics by as much as 3.79. Muon is not used because the fixed-effect vector has no natural matrix axis, its apparent matrix shape would couple unrelated mice or depend on the arbitrary block-contrast basis. Momentum SGD, RMSProp, Lion, and related fixed-schedule first-order methods share Adam's batching advantage but not L-BFGS's line search or curvature history, so the failed Adam experiment provides no reason to replace the converged optimizer with them. Projecting a previously fitted alternative into later null models reduced iterations but also moved local optima. Running two independent L-BFGS workers reduced matched wall time by 38\% for mouse and 24\% for human, with greater peak memory and little reduction in aggregate CPU time.

Genome-wide shape census supports batched likelihood evaluation but not one globally padded tensor. Among 4,825 mouse and 11,597 human block tests, only 813 and 434 belong to an exact tensor-shape group of size at least two. Allowing pseudobulk and mouse counts to vary while holding the isoform, EC, and fixed-effect dimensions constant raises these counts to 3,726 and 7,971. Cost-sorted padded batches incur estimated likelihood-work inflation of 1.97-fold for mouse and 2.11-fold for human at batch size eight, increasing with batch size. On representative low-padding batches, a padded likelihood-gradient kernel was 2.5-fold faster than a \texttt{ragged\_dot} kernel for mouse and 2.2-fold faster for human. On deliberately high-padding tails, the ragged kernel was 1.09-fold and 1.86-fold faster and compiled faster.

A full padded Laplace prototype gives every fit an independent L-BFGS history, projected gradient, active state, and Armijo line search while evaluating the separable objectives together. It preserves implicit differentiation of each posterior mode and reuse of a shared unrestricted alternative. In ordinary low-dimensional buckets, batches of eight were 5.48-fold faster for mouse and 6.43-fold faster for human than sequential scalar L-BFGS-B. All eight batched nested fits converged in each dataset, compared with seven mouse and eight human scalar fits. Among fits converged by both implementations, the maximum absolute LRT-statistic differences were 0.0090 and 0.00058. A four-fit high-dimensional mouse bucket was 1.53-fold faster after alternative reuse and gave identical statistics. A high-dimensional human bucket with ten isoforms, 18 null coefficients, 252 alternative coefficients, and roughly 900 pseudobulks was 0.62-fold as fast, meaning batching was 1.62-fold slower; the two commonly converged tests differed by 6.88 and 14.80 statistic units because the optimizers reached different local optima. Active masks preserve accepted fits but do not remove the arithmetic of inactive padded lanes from a compiled vectorized evaluation.

The initial selective policy was tested on every cell-type block, using cost-sorted batches of eight only for padding ratios at most two and nested models with at most 128 outer coefficients. The mouse hybrid reduced elapsed worker time only from 31.18 to 30.48 hours, while the human hybrid increased it from 98.39 to 125.21 hours. Peak worker memory fell from 3.97 to 1.40 GiB in mouse and from 6.55 to 4.19 GiB in human. The memory reduction did not compensate for the human slowdown or the movement between local optima. Production scalar fitting preserves bounded compilation reuse within a gene and covered pseudobulk set and initializes the first unrestricted alternative from its fitted null. The initial global shape-and-cost scheduler instead broke gene-local compilation reuse and fit deduplicated alternatives from their default initialization. The latter difference moved optima even on routes where both fits were scalar, while batching introduced additional trajectories. That initial hybrid was rejected without structured-null calibration because it failed the prior runtime and numerical-agreement gates.

A follow-up restored the production null-to-alternative initialization and made counts, EC maps, fixed-effect tensors, and cluster layouts dynamic arguments to a bounded global shape cache. On 20 real mouse tests, global caching reduced elapsed time by 10.3\% and objective-evaluation time by 9.7\% with identical statistics, at the cost of 11.0\% greater peak memory, so this bounded cache remains available. Reusing each accepted L-BFGS iterate's posterior mode with adaptive Newton termination halved optimizer time, but maximum statistic drift remained 0.319 at mode tolerance $10^{-5}$ and 0.187 at $10^{-8}$. A plug-in raw-EC efficient-score screen was slower than the full nested LRT because forming the observed outer Hessian dominated its cost; seven of 20 fixed-effect Hessians were indefinite, and one extreme score disagreed with the LRT tail. The production-initialized GPU scheduler later reduced mouse worker time by 21.3\% but increased human worker time by 36.9\%, increased peak memory in both datasets, and retained local-optimum drift. Adaptive mode reuse, the score screen, and batched schedulers were therefore removed rather than exposed as analysis options.

The multinomial EC likelihood permits a cheaper exact derivative kernel than nested automatic differentiation. For one pseudobulk and primer, let $\bm y\in\mathbb{N}^{E}$ contain counts for $E$ equivalence classes, let $M\in\{0,1\}^{E\times K}$ map those classes to $K$ isoforms, let $\bm a=\operatorname{softmax}([\bm\eta^T,0]^T)\in\mathbb{R}^{K}$ be isoform abundance for free logits $\bm\eta\in\mathbb{R}^{K-1}$, let $B=M\operatorname{diag}(\bm a)\in\mathbb{R}^{E\times K}$, let $\bm m=B\bm 1_K\in\mathbb{R}^{E}$, let $D=\bm 1_E^T\bm m$, let $N=\bm 1_E^T\bm y$, and let $\bm c=B^T\bm 1_E\in\mathbb{R}^{K}$. Ignoring the multinomial coefficient, the log likelihood is \[\ell=\bm y^T\log\bm m-N\log D.\] Its full-logit score $\bm g\in\mathbb{R}^{K}$ and Hessian $H\in\mathbb{R}^{K\times K}$ are \[\bm g=B^T(\bm y\oslash\bm m)-N\bm c/D,\qquad H=\operatorname{diag}(\bm g)-B^T\operatorname{diag}(\bm y\oslash\bm m^2)B+N\bm c\bm c^T/D^2.\] Deleting the reference isoform's last matrix row and column gives derivatives with respect to $\bm\eta$, and derivatives sum over primers. Substituting these expressions for the pseudobulk-level autodiff score and Hessian leaves the Laplace objective, posterior-mode solve, implicit mode gradient, bounds, and L-BFGS convergence criteria unchanged while avoiding differentiation through an autodiff-generated Hessian.

On matched subsets of 12 mouse and 10 human tests, the analytic kernel reduced end-to-end time by factors of 1.49 and 1.48 and objective-evaluation time by factors of 1.24 and 1.48. Joint convergence was unchanged at 11 and 9 tests. Median absolute LRT differences were $2.4\times10^{-11}$ and $2.3\times10^{-13}$, and maxima were 0.040 and 0.124. Peak memory fell from 2.35 to 1.33 GiB in mouse and from 4.81 to 4.32 GiB in human.

Table~\ref{tab:analytic-derivative-full} gives the complete scalar reruns for both contrasts. Analytic derivatives and reduced repeated compilation lowered cell-type aggregate worker time, including merging, by factors of 2.48 in mouse and 1.78 in human and condition time by factors of 2.28 and 2.44. Mouse condition joint convergence and all three BH calls were identical. Human condition gained three joint convergences, and all 193 BH calls were identical. Median condition LRT drift was near floating-point precision, with maxima of 0.229 in mouse and 3.03 in human. Across both contrasts, broad worker time fell from 118.8 to 51.0 hours in mouse and from 729.4 to 313.3 hours in human. Broad peak memory changed from 4.74 to 4.81 GiB in mouse and from 6.83 to 8.25 GiB in human, where the peak occurs in the cell-type rather than condition run. The analytic derivative kernel with gene-grouped L-BFGS is therefore the production scalar implementation; condition discovery counts and the biological conclusions are unchanged.

Mouse & Condition & 14,833 & 13,779/13,779 & 3/3 & 3 & 1.000 & $2.27\times10^{-13}$ & 0.229 & 38.41/87.60 & 4.81/4.74\\
Human & Cell type & 11,597 & 10,982/10,981 & 1,463/1,459 & 1,458 & 0.996 & $1.40\times10^{-9}$ & 26.68 & 55.17/98.39 & 8.25/6.55\\
Human & Condition & 73,144 & 68,774/68,771 & 193/193 & 193 & 1.000 & $9.09\times10^{-13}$ & 3.03 & 258.10/631.05 & 6.16/6.83\\

\end{table}

A bounded trust-region Newton--CG alternative used exact Hessian--vector products through the same Laplace objective. On one representative mouse test, 20 trust iterations took 90.4 seconds, including 67.3 seconds in 261 Hessian--vector products, and neither nested fit converged; L-BFGS converged in 40.5 seconds. The corresponding human comparison took 151.4 versus 54.3 seconds, with 125.4 seconds in 251 Hessian--vector products and neither trust fit converged. Larger matched trust runs were still unfinished after more than three times the analytic L-BFGS benchmark duration. Exact second-order outer optimization is therefore rejected because differentiating the implicit mode and log-determinant terms is more expensive than the saved iterations.

The production-initialized genome-wide scheduler uses batches of 32, requires at least four equal-parameter fits, admits padding ratios at most three and at most 128 outer coefficients, and sends every other shape to the same scalar Laplace LRT. Failed batched fits continue from their batched parameters and, if necessary, restart from the production initialization. Table~\ref{tab:hybrid-batched-full} gives the complete rerun. Mouse worker time fell 21.3\%, from 31.18 to 24.55 hours, while peak memory rose from 3.97 to 5.31 GiB. Human worker time rose 36.9\%, from 98.39 to 134.74 hours, and peak memory rose from 6.55 to 8.14 GiB. Only 1,344 of 11,597 human nulls met the batching gate; its 10,253 scalar nulls consumed 85.23 hours, so faster unrestricted alternatives could not recover the null-tail cost. The mouse and human discovery-set Jaccard indices against harmonized scalar production were 0.973 and 0.991. Median absolute LRT drift was small, 0.000131 and 0.000191, but maximum differences of 423.7 and 411.6 show that independently converged optimizers occasionally reached different local optima. A common production replacement is therefore rejected, and the reported results retain gene-grouped scalar fitting in both datasets.

Four multilevel mouse null families independently permuted the complete cell-type label vector within each subject while holding pseudobulk EC counts, nuisance columns, covered pseudobulks, and within-subject label counts fixed. Among 18,379 converged hybrid tests, rejection rates were 0.0983, 0.0385, and 0.0141 at nominal levels 0.05, 0.01, and 0.001, and every family produced 51--64 BH calls. An exact gene-grouped scalar rerun of the first permutation gave rates 0.1047, 0.0432, and 0.0148 and 62 BH calls, compared with 64 hybrid calls, 60 shared, and discovery Jaccard 0.909. Thus batching does not cause the null inflation, but the ordinary multilevel omnibus chi-square LRT is not calibrated and its BH counts in the matched method comparison must be treated as descriptive discovery yield rather than controlled FDR. This finding does not apply to the separately bootstrap-calibrated primary mouse analysis or the paired-label pairwise benchmark.

Adding the event-based baselines does not change the shared-universe comparison. The original event-universe SUPPA2 split audit has 4,204 genes represented in both halves, but no gene passes the two-fold conjunction BH threshold; its fold log-p correlation is 0.207. The matched-split SUPPA2 gene--cell-type-pair aggregation is the row in Table~\ref{tab:ec-glmm-reproducibility}. The historical unpaired rMATS audit contributes two fold-level BH genes in each half but no replicated gene on its 496-gene shared universe, with held-half nominal replication 0.000 and fold log-p correlation 0.530. The full-data paired-JCEC rMATS run restores 1,497 event FDR calls and is included in the long-read comparison above. The separately refitted paired subject-half comparison replaces the historical row in the main reproducibility table. Detailed tables, paired tests, and the event-catalog audit are in \texttt{analyses/comparator\_suppa\_rmats/suppa2}.
